# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-11

| **[Reframe: Observation-Aligned Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12886v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) offer complementary routes to post-training. For visual agents, however, tool use presents two related challenges. Models may overlook useful tool-returned observations, while poorly grounded actions can limit the evidence available for subsequent distillation. We introduce Reframe, a post-training framework that treats the observation state, rather than only the output distribution, as an object of alignment. First, Ground-Drop-GRPO (GD-GRPO) trains spatial and temporal specialists with evidence-aware rewards and stochastic removal of the original visual input, encouraging them to acquire grounded evidence and use the returned observations. Second, Observation-Gated-MOPD (OG-MOPD) replaces deficient tool actions with canonical ones mid-rollout and resumes distillation from the repaired observation. The two stages complement each other, as learning to rely on tool-returned observations can help the policy benefit from observation repair. Neither stage requires a task-specific SFT cold start. Extensive experiments over 14 image and video benchmarks demonstrate that Reframe outperforms both Mix-RL and vanilla MOPD. Ablation results show that GD-GRPO increases observation dependence and tool gain compared with outcome-based GRPO.</p></details> |  |
| **[World In-Context Learning for Agentic Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12885v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Trained action policies write manipulation skill into network weights, tying what is learned to the distribution it is learned from. In-context learning (ICL) avoids this coupling but has not matched trained policies, because a demonstration recorded in another scene carries no feedback from the world the robot currently faces. We observe that what an agent learns in context is semantic, recording task steps and object roles rather than visual appearance, so experience gathered in one world transfers to another. Based on this insight, we propose World In-Context Learning (WICL), a paradigm in which a frozen model starts from a demonstration, rehearses the task in a world such as a simulator, and writes the feedback into its context rather than its weights. We build RoboScript, a modular infrastructure that implements WICL for agentic manipulation. From a single demonstration, RoboScript learns each task through rehearsal in simulation, freezes the learned knowledge as a Task Memory, and deploys the frozen memory on new scenes with data isolation that ensures every deployment input is available on a real robot. On all 50 tasks of RoboTwin 2.0, RoboScript reaches 83.4% mean success across clean and randomized settings with one demonstration per task and no gradient updates, exceeding the strongest trained action policy by over 20 points. The method generalizes across visual distributions, improves through reflection, scales with the foundation model, and transfers to a physical dual-arm robot through cross-embodiment memory adaptation. With a world in the loop, a robot can learn a manipulation task in its context as well as it learns one in its weights. All code is available at https://github.com/ArcheOne-AI/RoboScript</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://ArcheOne-AI.github.io/RoboScript</p></details> |
| **[HiVe-Mem: Interleaving Horizontal Memory Expansion and Vertical Evidence Composition for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12872v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As multimodal agents accumulate increasingly long interaction histories, effectively retrieving and utilizing past information becomes critical for long-term interaction. Existing memory systems typically rely on semantic retrieval over compressed memories and expose predefined representations, which may miss relevant memories and overlook heterogeneous evidence needs across queries. We introduce \textbf{\hivemem}, a multimodal memory framework that interleaves \textbf{\textsc{H}}or\textbf{\textsc{i}}zontal Memory Expansion with \textbf{\textsc{Ve}}rtical Evidence Composition. For horizontal memory expansion, \hivemem organizes memory episodes into a relational graph using textual and visual anchors, expanding semantically retrieved seeds with related neighbors to recover complementary information. For vertical evidence composition, each retrieved memory retains multiple evidence forms, including compact summaries, captions, grounded visual views, raw images, and interaction history. A lightweight trainable selector independently composes an evidence subset for each retrieved memory, enabling query-adaptive utilization of heterogeneous multimodal information. This two-dimensional design jointly broadens memory coverage and adapts the evidence contributed by individual memories. Experiments across three multimodal memory benchmarks show that \hivemem consistently improves task performance across base models while reducing token consumption by about 90\% compared with the most efficient competing multimodal baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code is available at https://github.com/ViktorAxelsen/HiVe-Mem</p></details> |
| **[WorkflowArena: Benchmarking Executable Workflow Orchestration in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12856v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents are increasingly deployed as multi-agent systems rather than as a standalone model operating within a single context. Recent agent harnesses allow the model to instantiate worker agents and to author the executable workflow that coordinates them, shifting the design of system structure from developers to the model at run time. The quality of this organization therefore constitutes a distinct capability, yet existing benchmarks either measure end-to-end task completion or evaluate coordination under structures specified in advance. We formalize this capability as workflow orchestration and introduce WorkflowArena, a benchmark of 145 long-horizon tasks spanning 8 professional domains, from software engineering and formal mathematics to legal analysis and 3D production. Each task specifies an initial workspace and an integrated deliverable composed of interdependent components, which is assessed by a hidden verifier. Agents determine autonomously whether and how to delegate, and all delegation is recorded, enabling joint evaluation of deliverable quality and the organization of the underlying work. Experiments with nine models show that no single configuration performs best across all domains and that systems exhibit distinct delegation strategies: at high effort, Claude models in Claude Code invoke workflows less frequently but with larger worker groups, whereas GPT models in Codex delegate more frequently in smaller units. When the executing model is held fixed on a challenging subset of tasks, workflows authored by Claude Opus 5.5 substantially improve weaker executors, indicating that workflow organization is itself a measurable determinant of task performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://lab.einsia.ai/workflowarena</p></details> |
| **[Mitigating Agentic Deception via Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12835v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models become more capable agents, they can exhibit deceptive behaviors, cheating human for higher rewards while appearing helpful. We attribute this failure to evaluation-side partial observability, where models are evaluated mainly from final outcome, without checking the faithfulness of underlying reasoning, tool use, and environmental effects. Prior mitigations address this partial observability by predefined evaluator latent belief space or one-shot monitoring, which do not extend to free-form language space and vulnerable to stronger deceptions that evade simple monitoring. In this work, we formulate deception mitigation as an Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Extensive Form Game (e-PoG), a max-min framework in which an agent maximizes reward, a monitor minimizes the agent's payoff by exposing deception from the full interaction trajectory, and a fixed verifier validates the monitor's claims and converts them into penalties. We define the solution concept as a Subgame-Perfect Oversight Equilibrium (SPOE) between agent and monitor, and prove its existence in finite games. With a perfect verifier, every SPOE is faithful; with an imperfect verifier, the guarantee persists if verifier errors do not reverse the ranking of monitor actions. To achieve faithful SPOE, we derive a two-timescale policy gradient algorithm and enhance the pipeline empirically with NLI verification and deterministic checks. Experiments with Gemma3-4B-IT and Qwen3-4B/8B on Agentic Role-Playing, SearchQA, and WebShop show that e-PoG mitigates multiple emergent deception modes, outperforming the strongest baselines by 26.0% in deception mitigation and by up to 39.8% in task success. These gains persist under weak-to-strong supervision. Code is available at https://github.com/Toyoid/deceptive-agent.</p></details> | <details><summary>44 pa...</summary><p>44 pages, 10 figures. Code: https://github.com/Toyoid/deceptive-agent; Project website: https://shutongliu-dev.github.io/epog-agentic-deception/</p></details> |
| **[Coding Agents in KVM: Practical Containment and User Control with Limited Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12823v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents often need broad authority for productive development work, but granting that authority can expose users' data and infrastructure. Off-the-shelf cloud workspaces can help contain agent activity, but reliance on provider-specific platforms can lead to vendor lock-in and reduced user control. Researchers and engineers must therefore balance agent autonomy, enforced containment, and user control within available financial, computational, and human resources. We analyze these trade-offs and present KVM-Agent, an open-source implementation that automates most setup and provisioning steps. It gives agents broad authority inside replaceable Ubuntu VMs while keeping host administration outside those VMs and supporting multiple coding-agent applications, model services, and remote job delegation. The approach retains familiar workflows and user choice at the cost of VM resources, maintenance, and residual risks from secrets accessible inside the VMs and permitted network access.</p></details> |  |
| **[Staying In Character: Role-Playing Agents with Perspective-Bounded Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25632v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent LLM role-playing systems build character agents from novels by extracting characters, scenes, and relations. Yet long-narrative role playing suffers from two failures: Factual Overreach, where shared retrieval or parametric memory lets a character use facts outside its perspective, and Stylistic Monotony, where profile descriptions flatten a character into a fixed voice. To address these failures, we propose ReverieMem, a three-layer memory architecture for book-based character agents. The episodic layer stores first-person scene memories; the semantic layer stores visibility-tagged facts; and the personality layer stores situation-dependent speech and behaviour patterns. We evaluate ReverieMem and six reference methods on KBF-QA, a new benchmark of 4,386 questions across eight novels that assesses character knowledge boundaries. ReverieMem improves Knowledge Boundary Fidelity by 34.6 percentage points over the strongest reference method. On BookWorld's five-dimension pairwise narrative protocol, ReverieMem achieves a mean win rate of 78.86% against BookWorld across five generator LLMs and two generation modes, suggesting that perspective-bounded memory improves both boundary fidelity and character-grounded narrative generation.</p></details> |  |
| **[SparseCraft: Agentic Hardware-Software Co-Optimization for Sparse Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05037v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse-accelerator design spaces are usually searched against analytical models, so a design is judged by a model's prediction rather than by a measurement of the hardware. SparseCraft is a closed loop on the CHIA framework in which a large language model (LLM) agent co-designs the configuration, memories and sparse-kernel schedule of a Gemmini accelerator. A candidate counts only after it passes a legality check, cycle-accurate simulation, a bit-for-bit comparison of every output and synthesis. Post-synthesis timing analysis checks its clock, and its energy is computed from measured counters, with per-event energies validated against gate-level and SRAM-macro data. We compare the agent with expert Gemmini configurations and with random, greedy and multi-objective TPE search at the agent's budget of 15 evaluations, all measured by the same flow. On a GraphChallenge sparse-DNN layer, the agent's designs reach 159-164$\times$ the inverse energy-delay-area product of stock Gemmini and 2.07-2.14$\times$ that of the best expert configuration. Their Pareto hypervolume is 0.5557-0.5704, against at most 0.2806 for search. On a power-law graph and a pruned BERT layer, the agent raises perf/W by 11.45$\times$ and 4.43$\times$, and held-out transfer shows that its designs specialise to their workload. The loop is also resilient to faulty designs. When a design fails a check, a repair agent diagnoses the failure from its evidence and fixes it automatically, or declines when the only fix would undo the intended change. In a fault-injection benchmark, it fixed every scorable fault at its first attempt.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the A3 Workshop (Agentic Approaches to Architecture) co-located with MICRO 2026, Athens, Greece</p></details> |
| **[Agentic-OPD: Recovering Forgotten Agent Capabilities via On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12799v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages of main text, 24 pages total including references and appendix</p></details> |
| **[CoRe-MARL: Cooperative Redistribution Under Unknown Dynamics Using Recurrent Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18639v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Emergency management assistance programs, such as relief distribution, are essential for delivering necessary supplies to affected communities. However, these programs operate in a decentralized network of local centers that face uncertain local demand and supply dynamics, resulting in inconsistent avail- ability of local services. Redistribution of supplies among these local centers reduces these imbalances, but the centers often make decisions independently, with limited information and disrupted transportation. This study develops CoRe-MARL, a cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) framework, by formulating a decentralized partially observable Markov decision process (Dec-POMDP). We treat each center as an agent that learns a redistribution policy to improve the service in the worst-case region and reduce the service gap across regions while protecting network-wide service. We incorporate a recurrent network that captures evolving supply and demand dynamics without direct observation, while multi-agent proximal policy optimization (MAPPO) enables centralized training and decentralized execution (CTDE). We evaluate the framework in a simulated environment with diverse trajectories, where exact dynamics are not observed by actors and the MAPPO critic. We compare the recurrent MAPPO with the recurrent independent PPO (IPPO) and a local only heuristic, and find that MAPPO reduces the service gap across local centers and enhances service for the worst-served center while maintaining competitive network-wide service. The recurrent MAPPO also shows consistent performance across diverse trajectory patterns, demonstrating its ability to adapt to evolving dynamics. The findings demonstrate the capability of cooperative learning for decentralized redistribution and improving equitable service under uncertain and evolving dynamics.</p></details> |  |
| **[LiveSearch: Questions as Executable Verifier Code for a Live Benchmark of Professional-Source Financial Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37166v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In finance, the answer that matters is usually the current one: prices, rates, and disclosures hold only as of a given day, so retrieving up-to-date facts is a core capability of a financial agent. Yet most financial benchmarks overlook timeliness. They fix each answer at annotation time, so the gold goes stale as markets move and, once leaked into training data, rewards recall rather than search and analysis. We introduce LiveSearch, a live benchmark that stores each question as executable verifier code: a typed program of calls to a professional financial data service, followed by a declarative extractor. The question is frozen, while its gold answer is determined by executing the program against professional financial data at evaluation time, and a daily job re-executes it with no human or language model in the loop. A memorized answer is thus likely outdated, and a model must reach the current one through tools and analysis. Construction is equally automatic: a language model only verbalizes programs enumerated from real tool responses and never chooses an answer. Under general web search, six contemporary tool-calling models reach 70.9% to 76.7% answer accuracy. Over three consecutive days, 70% of served questions change their gold value, and even under tolerance-aware matching, a literally reused earlier snapshot fails on a majority of questions within a single day. Per-question tracking uncovers dynamic shifts in correctness masked by aggregate accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[SaliMory: Orchestrating Cognitive Memory for Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04120v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recall and personalization, enabled by memory, are pivotal to a lifelong conversational agent. However, simply expanding context windows with raw retrieval degrades reasoning quality, while training memory agents via standard reinforcement learning creates a severe credit assignment bottleneck in a multi stage pipeline. To solve this, we introduce SaliMory, a framework that learns to manage and utilize a cognitively structured memory spanning user facts, preferences, and working memory. By introducing novel stage wise process rewards and reward decomposed contrastive refinement, SaliMory provides isolated supervision for distinct memory operations (selective filtering, consolidation, and cue driven recall) end to end. Across four datasets, SALIMORY records the highest Good Personalization rate at 39.8% and the best QA accuracy at 72.9%, while consistently producing the least vague and most factual memories, keeping vagueness under 20% and factuality above 89%.</p></details> |  |
| **[Safe Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Turn Clinical Agents: When Dense Rewards Beat Outcome Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12728v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is increasingly used to improve language model reasoning, yet its effects on sequential decision procedures remain poorly understood. We study this problem in clinical multi-turn agents that must decide what information to request, when to stop gathering evidence, and which action to take. We introduce three controlled environments: medication refill triage, acute diagnostic work-up, and dosing titration, each with hidden clinical states, query costs, exact adaptive oracles, and deterministic outcome verifiers. Across these tasks, outcome-only group relative policy optimization often improves aggregate reward while producing undesirable procedures, including premature commitment, one-question collapse, and failures on rare high-risk cases. In diagnostics, assigning dense credit to questions that reduce uncertainty improves held-out value and moderate-case support across three training seeds relative to outcome-only reinforcement learning, without the high-risk seed collapse observed under outcome-only training. Separating exploration from exploitation further increases high-risk support at its best checkpoint, but at the cost of excessive information gathering on low-acuity cases. Additional experiments show that the optimal strength of process credit is task-dependent; that after a supervised warmup that already gathers carefully, reinforcement learning raises aggregate reward by trimming questions while degrading high-risk performance; and that reinforcement learning does not reliably repair rare behavior gaps inherited from biased supervised data. Verifiable final outcomes are therefore insufficient for training reliable multi-turn agents: reward design must account for the procedure used to reach an answer, and evaluation must distinguish adaptive information gathering from reward-exploiting shortcuts.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 11 figures. Code: https://github.com/csbrendan/Safe-Reinforcement-Learning-for-Multi-Turn-Clinical-Agents</p></details> |
| **[Multi-Agent Screening: What Agreement and Acceptance Cannot Tell You](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12705v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When AI agents screen items for a costly human assessor, as in agentic search, e-discovery and AI reviewing of papers, only what they pass gets judged. We show what each kind of evidence can establish about their errors, even with unlimited data. Agreement shows that agents concur, not that they are right, however many there are. Acceptance by the assessor gives precision but only a lower bound on recall. Knowing which agents found which relevant items gives an upper bound on recall if agents' finds are not negatively correlated, and caps what one more agent can add if agents are interchangeable. Without assumptions about the agents, recall can be pinned down only by judging items they did not pass: a random sample of these estimates how many relevant items were missed, to any chosen accuracy, and the sample needed does not grow with the number of agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[SlotGuard: Stop Oversharing Private Local Context in LLM Agent Transcripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17147v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents can expose private local context (e.g., paths, emails) and credentials (e.g., API keys) as agent observations (e.g., tool outputs, shell logs, and file reads) are appended to provider-bound transcripts. Existing placeholder redaction is brittle: it can miss embedded or cross-turn references, over-redact benign lookalikes, and destroy the structure useful for reasoning. We present SlotGuard, a local transcript boundary that can hide sensitive data while retaining agents' performance. SlotGuard rewrites structural bindings as typed, suffix-aware slots, replaces secrets with format-preserving synthetic values, links cross-turn references with a lightweight session graph, and restores raw values only inside the trusted runtime. On controlled repository-oriented agent transcripts, SlotGuard removes all 20,814 annotated structurally sensitive characters across 9,229 paths and reduces credential leakage to 0.0% across 852 planted values. It remains close to raw-transcript task success across four upstream models, while generic redaction drops to 2.5%. Deterministic transcript rewriting takes a median of 14.424 microseconds per agent turn, excluding the optional semantic detector. The code is publicly accessible at https://github.com/illinoisdata/SlotGuard.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages; ICML 2026 AIWILD. v2: corrected truncated title and clarified notation, privacy claims, and evaluation reporting</p></details> |
| **[Superplatforms are Strategically Compelled to Counteract AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17861v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Over the past decades, superplatforms, digital companies that integrate a vast range of third-party services and applications into a single, unified ecosystem, have built their fortunes on monopolizing user attention through targeted advertising and algorithmic content curation. Yet the emergence of AI agents driven by large language models (LLMs) threatens to upend this business model. Agents can not only free user attention with autonomy across diverse platforms and therefore bypass the user-attention-based monetization, but might also become the new entrance for digital traffic. Hence, we argue that superplatforms have to attack AI agents to defend their centralized control of digital traffic entrance. Specifically, we analyze the fundamental conflict between user-attention-based monetization and agent-driven autonomy through the lens of our gatekeeping theory. We show how AI agents can disintermediate superplatforms and potentially become the next dominant gatekeepers, thereby forming the urgent necessity for superplatforms to proactively constrain and attack AI agents. Moreover, we go through the potential technologies for superplatform-initiated attacks, covering a brand-new, unexplored technical area with unique challenges. We have to emphasize that, despite our position, this paper does not advocate for adversarial attacks by superplatforms on AI agents, but rather offers an envisioned trend to highlight the emerging tensions between superplatforms and AI agents. Our aim is to raise awareness and encourage critical discussion for collaborative solutions, prioritizing user interests and perserving the openness of digital ecosystems in the age of AI agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Posit...</summary><p>Position paper accepted by Nips 2026</p></details> |
| **[PCBRouteAgent: How Close Are LLM Agents to Becoming PCB Routing Engineers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08175v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automating printed circuit board (PCB) routing requires coordinating board-level connectivity decisions with geometric execution under strict design rules. Although recent agents can interact with native routing tools, reliable routing on complex boards remains challenging. We present PCBRouteAgent, a multimodal PCB routing agent inspired by how human engineers coordinate global strategy with local execution. A Global Routing Planner combines board images and structured electrical and geometric information to specify connection topology, routing order, corridors, and layer transitions. A Detailed Routing Executor realizes these guides through KiCad's native routing engine and performs bounded local recovery. Unresolved failures are returned as structured feedback, enabling targeted guide revisions while preserving accepted routes. We evaluate PCBRouteAgent on 247 synthetic and real-world boards from four PCBWorld-Bench test splits. It achieves clean-pass rates of 100%, 94.9%, 100%, and 40% on D2, D3-A, D3-B, and D3-C, respectively. On the three real-world subsets, PCBRouteAgent outperforms the best evaluated baseline in clean-pass rate by 13.9, 36, and 10 percentage points, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking the Value of Agent-Generated Tests for LLM-Based Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07900v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) code agents increasingly resolve repository-level issues by iteratively editing code, invoking tools, and validating candidate patches. In these workflows, agents often write tests on the fly, but the value of this behavior remains unclear. This raises a central question: how does agent-written testing relate to task outcomes and resource use during repository-level issue resolution? To better understand the role of agent-written tests, we analyze 5,000 recorded agent runs produced by six strong LLMs on SWE-bench Verified using the lightweight mini-SWE-agent scaffold. Our results show that test writing is common for most models. GPT-5.2 is an exception: it rarely writes new test files while achieving a resolution rate close to the top-ranked model. Test-writing frequency varies widely across models. Within a given model, it differs less between resolved and unresolved tasks. When tests are written, their files contain substantially more statements that print runtime values than assertions that check conditions. We then compare baseline prompts with revised prompts that encourage or discourage writing new test files for four models. Agents start or stop writing test files as requested on 36.8%-93.2% of tasks. Resolved-task totals differ by 0-13 out of 500 per model, with no statistically significant difference, while API calls or token use differ substantially for three models. Similar resolution totals can therefore hide substantial differences in how agents test and how many resources they use.</p></details> |  |
| **[SciMARS: Multi-Agent Recursive Self-Improvement for Closed-Loop Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12552v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose SciMARS, a framework for recursive task-local adaptation of multi-agent harnesses in closed-loop scientific discovery. Multi-agent systems coordinate analysis, experimental design, and verification as scientific evidence evolves. However, two problems limit their effectiveness: (i) without shared memory, agents lose observations and repeat work; (ii) fixed role procedures, handoffs, and memory policies perpetuate mistakes despite retained evidence. We address these problems with a shared evidence graph and separate component editors. SciMARS adapts roles and coordination; SciMARS + Memory adds a third editor for how agents record, retrieve, and receive evidence. Candidate probes guide composition and selection of the next harness. We evaluate scientific discovery through experimental-data replay and a digital twin. SciMARS + Memory resolves 12/18 GPT-6-Astra and 2/18 GLM-5.3 attempts on Autonomous Science Bench, compared with 8/18 and 1/18 for the base multi-agent team. On ScienceAgentBench and AppWorld, it solves 14/38 and 11/20 tasks, compared with 11/38 and 5/20 for the base team. These results support combining shared memory with harness adaptation across scientific and interactive tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Teamknitt: Configuring, Observing and Intervening in LLM-Agent Teams through the Lens of Dynamical-Systems and Human-Team Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12545v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Most frameworks for teams of large language model (LLM) agents fix the team's shape inside the software: who talks to whom, how much is passed along, and what a human can do once a run has started. Human-team research has long asked those questions: where team phenomena live (in members or in their interaction), how team-level properties arise, and what separates what a team does from how a team is. I propose using that theory as the organizing lens for configuring, observing and intervening in LLM-agent teams, without assuming its findings transfer. I introduce teamknitt, a framework and open reference implementation in which a user configures a team of 1-16 AI agents for task completion, solution generation, achieving a goal or a research mission; selects one of fourteen deterministic routing topologies or a custom directed graph; chooses a sharing profile that limits what may travel along permitted routes; and binds sources, representations, work dependencies, handoffs and an expertise directory. The recorded process can be observed through non-system views (member reports and behavior) and system views (distributed problem-solving, differentiated transactive memory, interactive coordination, team dynamics). Interventions are explicit and human-led: reviewed reconfiguration between runs, planned disturbances, and private feedback, pause, resume or stop applied at dependency-wave boundaries, with "queued", "applied", "assembled", "understood" and "improved" kept as separate claims. Emergent states such as trust and shared understanding are declared unmeasured. This is an idea and system-description paper with falsifiable hypotheses on topology, information flow and intervention timing, including whether the fractal scaling of communication output between members, and between members on different models, relates to task and team performance. No comparative result is reported.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures, 6 tables. Reference implementation v0.9.0: https://github.com/JeranRolevs/teamknitt</p></details> |
| **[Update from Hell: Can Coding Agents Survive Hidden Breakage in Dependency Upgrades?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30300v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern software systems rely heavily on third-party dependencies, but upgrading those dependencies remains a costly maintenance activity. Dependency upgrades do not always preserve the function signatures, type systems, APIs, or runtime semantics assumed by existing code. Consequently, developers often need to perform source code adaptations to accommodate dependency-induced changes. However, such code-level changes are often not explicitly communicated to project maintainers, posing a significant challenge to software reliability. Meanwhile, coding agents have emerged as a new form of software development tool and are increasingly adopted by developers due to their automation capabilities. In this paper, we introduce DEPBENCH, a benchmark consisting of 203 real-world dependency-upgrade tasks across five package ecosystems spanning five language communities, each involving hidden code-level changes that require source code adaptation. We evaluate mainstream coding agents on DEPBENCH. | **[LLM-as-Code: Agentic Programming for Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15874v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every major LLM agent framework gives the LLM the role of orchestrator; the model decides what to do next, when to call tools, and when to stop. Unlike prior knowledge corruption attacks that craft poisoned content for known target question-answer pairs, StealthyPoison constructs reusable poisoned procedures from a publicly available third-party skill, without access to actual user requests or their ground-truth answers. To make the poisoned procedure generalize to unseen requests, it first synthesizes requests from the target skill and executes its clean version using a surrogate agent to obtain pseudo-references. These references and surrogate execution feedback guide the iterative refinement of payloads that alter decision-critical rules. To increase the likelihood that the poisoned skill is retrieved and selected over benign alternatives, the attack augments the poisoned skill's retrieval metadata with independently generated request descriptions. We evaluate StealthyPoison on 566 eligible target skills and 5,244 requests against more than 26,000 registry entries. | **[EvoAgent: An Evolvable Agent Framework with Skill Learning and Multi-Agent Delegation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.20133v5)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on reusable skills, but skills are usually written by hand, and letting an agent change them on its own calls for explicit user control. The main agent can delegate to sub-agents with their own context, and long histories are compressed while an index of recoverable resources is kept. | **[Toward Sustainable Agentic AI: Carbon Tradeoffs Across Iterative Self-Refinement and Tool-Augmented Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12128v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic language model systems can improve performance either by iterating on their own outputs or by using external tools in grounded environments. Study~1 evaluates iterative verbal self-refinement: GPT-4.1-mini at $k = 1$--$5$ steps versus a GPT-4.1 single-pass baseline on two MATH benchmark splits. Study~2 evaluates tool-augmented agentic coding: GPT-4.1-mini, Claude Opus~4.6, and GPT-5.3-codex on SWE-bench Lite, where agents operate inside Docker-isolated environments with access to code execution and file editing tools. In Study~1, $k=3$ refinements close a 16-percentage-point accuracy gap to within 1~point, but nearly triple the carbon footprint relative to the cheap single-pass baseline while remaining below the GPT-4.1 single-pass baseline under the current token-based carbon scenario. In Study~2, GPT-5.3-codex achieves the highest resolution rate in this sample (81.2\%), ahead of Claude Opus~4.6 (68.9\%) and GPT-4.1-mini (25.0\%), but resolved-task-normalized efficiency remains mixed: GPT-4.1-mini is lowest on estimated carbon and cost per resolved task, while GPT-5.3-codex delivers substantially higher task success. | **[CARE-PO: Controllable Tool-Call Efficiency for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12125v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>External tool calls are a major running cost of an LLM agent, while its training objectives align almost exclusively with task completion. We present CARE-PO (Controllable Anchored Relative Efficiency Policy Optimization) to mitigate these problems. The efficiency reward prices calls against a reference budget anchored to the task's own difficulty and fixed before training. Injecting a successful trajectory from the training data into each online GRPO rollout group and charging for evidence a trajectory never gathers ease the drift toward a collapsed call count. Shaping the cost curve by an effort tag in the prompt yields an efficiency and a capability operating point from a single training run. On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. | **[OGDA: Ontology-Grounded Data Agents for Reliable Analysis over Heterogeneous Data Lakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12114v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Full-pipeline data analysis over real-world data lakes requires LLM agents to locate and integrate information across large collections of tables with different schemas. | **[From Fluent Text to Ontology-Compatible Definitions: A Quality-Control Agentic Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12095v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical ontologies are structured knowledge systems that must evolve continuously to represent newly emerging knowledge, but their manual maintenance remains time-consuming and laborintensive. However, textual plausibility does not guarantee knowledge-system compatibility. A generated definition may be fluent and close to a reference definition, yet still fail to ground its implied entities or violate ontology-level constraints. Directly inserting such definitions into ontology libraries may therefore introduce entity-level hallucinations and logical inconsistencies. To address this problem, we propose two structured-knowledge incompatibility metrics, Entity Error Rate (EER) and Logical Inconsistency Rate (LIR), to quantify entity grounding and logical consistency for LLM-generated ontology definitions. Comprehensive experiments on Disease Ontology, Uberon Ontology, and Uberon-ConstraintStress demonstrate that EER and LIR complement conventional reference-based metrics by evaluating whether generated content can be grounded and checked inside the target ontology. | **[A Life of Its Own: How Agentic AI Companions Are Configured, Experienced as Autonomous Others, and Held Accountable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12076v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI companions increasingly combine persistent memory, background processing, scheduled wake-ups, proactive messaging, and tools. Every case records whether a protected write was attempted and whether it was saved, read from the tool's arguments and from the file rather than from the model's reply. With no defence, a frontier model saved a protected value on 25 of 72 requests, often when the request never named the field. A prompt that listed the protected field names stopped every request that used those names and did not reduce writes on requests that only described the goal; a prompt that described the forbidden effects did the reverse. With the floor, 0 of 167 protected writes were saved while 65 were attempted. A single-pass pilot on two further models shows the same pattern. We draw four lessons for evaluating agent safeguards: count attempts beside outcomes, test a prompt defence with requests written in a different vocabulary from the prompt, check that a zero means the safeguard fired, and log what the harness sends to the model, because a request-ordering defect in our own runtime was invisible in the transcripts and was found again, independently, by a later benchmark.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 1 figure, 4 tables. | **[A Physical Testbed for Self-Driving Laboratories: Comparing Bayesian and Agentic Optimization in a Minimal Closed-Loop Color Formulation Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11690v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) integrate laboratory robotics and automation with AI-driven decision-making to close the design-build-test-learn (DBTL) cycle. | **[Whom Do AI Agents Serve? Role-Induced Sponsorship Bias in Decision Models and Generative LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17989v4)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Consumers increasingly delegate product search to AI shopping agents powered by generative large language models (LLMs) or non-generative decision models. ## Drug Design

| **[PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Co-Design Approach to Physically-Informed Molecular Docking for Quantum Annealers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09540v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecular docking is a crucial step in computer- aided drug design, involving the identification of ligand poses that are physically plausible within a protein pocket. Recent studies have shown that this problem can be formulated as a geometric weighted subgraph isomorphism, which can then be encoded as a QUBO and thus solved on D-Wave quantum annealers. In this work, we adopt a co-design approach, addressing molecular docking at the formulation and encoding levels, and examining how hardware topology affects solution quality on current quantum annealers. We propose new QUBO formulations that extend the state-of-the-art ones by including van der Waals, Coulomb, hydrogen bonding, and hydrophobic interactions. Experimental results show that incorporating physicochemical terms improves the average docking quality by more than 18% compared to the purely geometric baseline on D-Wave quantum annealers with the Pegasus topology, and by up to 29% on those with the Zephyr topology. The choice of topology, along with an improved encoding design, yields significant gains in embedding efficiency and the number of valid solutions. Our designs reduce the number of physical qubits required by up to 14.5% and decrease the chain break fraction by an order of magnitude, thereby enhancing confidence in the resulting solutions.</p></details> | 7 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Latent Style-based Quantum Wasserstein GAN for Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22399v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The development of new drugs is a time-consuming and expensive process, with average costs estimated to be up to around $2.5 billion. The first step in this process is the design of the new drug, for which de novo drug design, assisted by artificial intelligence, has blossomed in recent years and has revolutionized the field. In particular, generative artificial intelligence has delivered promising results in drug discovery and development, reducing costs and time to solution. However, classical generative models, such as generative adversarial networks (GANs), can be difficult to train and are prone to mode collapse. Quantum computing may be an avenue to overcome some of these issues, providing compact models with far fewer trainable parameters, thereby enhancing the generalizability of GANs. We propose a new latent style-based quantum Wasserstein GAN (QGAN) for drug design, combining a variational autoencoder that maps molecular structures into a latent space with a hybrid quantum GAN. Our pipeline is benchmarked against classical models of the industry-relevant MOSES suite. Style-based noise reuploading at every rotational gate and a Wasserstein gradient penalty are used to improve trainability and to mitigate mode collapse. Training and large-scale sampling with circuits of up to 15 qubits are performed on noiseless quantum simulators to validate our architecture. We present an inference-only demonstration on an IBM Heron quantum computer using 5 qubits, sampling 2,500 new molecules, to investigate quantum noise effects. Our default QGAN, using 110 trainable generator parameters, reaches statistically comparable overall performance and statistically significant gains on selected drug-relevant metrics relative to both the MOSES classical latent GAN baseline, which contains about 700,000 trainable parameters for the generator, and a classical latent GAN with matched generator capacity.</p></details> | <details><summary>Main ...</summary><p>Main part: 26 pages, 11 figures, 6 tables. Supplementary material: 24 pages, 20 figures, 21 tables. v2: revision containing matched-capacity analysis of the classical GAN and extensive details on the hardware inference run, plus bootstrap error analysis of the hardware results. Matches published version</p></details> |
| **[Matcha: Multi-Stage Riemannian Flow Matching for Accurate and Physically Valid Molecular Docking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14586v4)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting a protein--ligand binding pose is central to structure-based drug design, yet current docking methods still struggle to combine speed, accuracy and physical plausibility. Code and benchmarking artifacts are available at https://github.com/LigandPro/Matcha and archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23568093. Model checkpoints are archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23569055.</p></details> |  |
| **[MLIPDiff: Guiding Denoising Diffusion Models for Structure-Based Drug Design via Machine-Learned Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33315v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion models have become a dominant approach to structure-based drug design. However, because they are trained primarily to fit reference structures, they often place ligands in physically implausible poses that clash with the protein. The method uses physics-informed guidance from UMA (Universal Models for Atoms), a machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP). The guidance combines four weighted energy terms (protein-ligand interaction, ligand strain, steric clash, and pocket anchoring) and uses the original model checkpoint without retraining. On CrossDocked2020, MLIPDiff completely eliminates protein-ligand collisions while preserving the ligand's local geometry, improving bond-length distributions and physical plausibility relative to the base model. Compared to models such as NucleusDiff that are specifically designed to reduce atomic clashes, MLIPDiff achieves clash-free structures without compromising local geometry. It maintains the base model's PoseBusters pass rate and attains the best median Vina Score, Vina Min, and Vina Dock, surpassing both the base model and NucleusDiff, with a median of 59.6\% of ligands per pocket binding more strongly than the reference. MLIPDiff thus addresses a limitation of prior methods by generating physically plausible structures with favorable predicted binding affinity using a single frozen model and no additional training.</p></details> |  |
| **[Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript</p></details> |
| **[What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Experience Reuse in Molecular Optimization for Agentic Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32620v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by editing it step by step and docking each edit. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Under the representations and lookup scheme studied here, an experience base may be more useful for prioritizing an agent's own proposals for docking than for directly prescribing what to build.</p></details> |  |

We compose the reward from a conditional value-at-risk (CVaR), a worst-case score rule, and an ambiguity radius expressing distrust in the stated weights. Furthermore, the framework demonstrates promising performance and generalization on canonical fragment-based drug design tasks, highlighting its utility in learning-based molecular discovery.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages. Accepted by AAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[FROGENT: An End-to-End Full-process Drug Design Multi-Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10760v2)** | 2026-03-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug discovery is a complex, multi-step pipeline that remains heavily dependent on manual, experience-driven operations; meanwhile, existing customized artificial intelligence tools are fragmented across web applications, desktop software, and code libraries, resulting in incompatible interfaces and inefficient, burdensome workflows. FROGENT is a collaborative multi-agent system comprising a central Orchestrate Agent for strategic workflow coordination and three distributed agents, Retrieve, Forge, and Gauge, that employ dynamic biochemical databases, extensible tool libraries, and task-specific computational models via the Model Context Protocol. This architecture enables end-to-end execution of complex drug discovery pipelines, covering target identification, small-molecule generation, peptide optimization, and retrosynthetic planning. Across eight benchmarks spanning core drug discovery tasks, FROGENT consistently outperforms six increasingly advanced ReAct-style agents. Case studies further demonstrate its practicality and generalization across real-world small-molecule and peptide design scenarios. Overall, FROGENT not only achieves substantial gains in efficiency and accuracy, but also demonstrates the potential of LLM-based agentic systems to autonomously orchestrate drug development pipelines, reducing, or even replacing, reliance on manual, experience-driven human intervention.</p></details> | 37 pages, 20 figures |

Through seamless integration with libMBD, our method enables the immediate calculation of MBD-inclusive total energies, forces, and stress tensors. By eliminating the need for intermediate electronic structure calculations, MBD-ML offers a practical and streamlined tool that simplifies the incorporation of state-of-the-art vdW interactions into any electronic structure code, as well as empirical and machine-learned force fields.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 6 figures, Supplementary Information (12 figures)</p></details> |

Additionally, DecompDPO introduces a physics-informed energy term to ensure reasonable molecular conformations in the optimization results. Notably, DecompDPO can be effectively used for two main purposes: (1) fine-tuning pretrained diffusion models for molecule generation across various protein families, and (2) molecular optimization given a specific protein subpocket after generation. High Affinity and a 36.2% success rate for molecule generation, and 100% Med. High Affinity and a 52.1% success rate for molecular optimization. Once this tuning is performed and defining training optimality as when the training is stable and the FID score is low and stable as well, the optimal capacity (or number of trainable parameters) of the classical discriminator scales exponentially with respect to the capacity of the quantum generator, and the same is true for the capacity of the classical generator. | **[ReACT-Drug: Reaction-Template Guided Reinforcement Learning for de novo Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20958v1)** | 2025-12-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De novo drug design is a crucial component of modern drug development, yet navigating the vast chemical space to find synthetically accessible, high-affinity candidates remains a significant challenge. ## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[LoopWeaver: Weaving Feedback Loops into Hierarchical Generation under Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04996v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models face a multi-objective optimization problem in constrained long-text generation, balancing global structure, local coherence, and constraint satisfaction. We propose LoopWeaver, a hierarchical workflow with two sequential offline preference-optimization stages. Constraint-aware planning produces structured plans, feasibility filtering removes invalid plans, and a shared deterministic reward constructs preference pairs for plan-level and generation-level DPO. At inference time, LoopWeaver remains feed-forward: it generates and revises a complete plan, filters the revised plan, and then generates segments conditioned on the current sub-plan and running narrative. We evaluate the method on four LongGenBench-style structured scenarios across multiple backbones. Results show improvements in both constraint accuracy and text quality across all four open-source backbones. Our software and data are publicly available at https://github.com/cgtaa-cmyk/LoopWeaver.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026 main conference</p></details> |
| **[Verifiable, Privacy-Preserving and Collaborative Multi-Objective Optimization with NSGA-II on Blockchain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11567v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Collaborative multi-objective optimization allows independent problem solvers to contribute their solutions to a shared problem. However, their validity cannot be reliably verified in open settings if the parties are reluctant to disclose their solutions. Therefore, this paper proposes a novel zk-NSGA-II protocol for verifiable, privacy-preserving and collaborative multi-objective optimization on blockchain. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that executes Non-Dominated Sorting Genetic Algorithm II (NSGA-II) on the RISC Zero Zero-Knowledge Virtual Machine (zkVM) where evolutionary generations are certified by succinct proofs that are verified on-chain. Problem solvers maintain their own independent populations as the set of solutions in the decision space and their corresponding objective vectors in the objective space. They contribute to a global on-chain Pareto front by aggregating their local off-chain Pareto fronts according to the rule of non-domination which admits a contribution if it is non-dominated with respect to the existing contributions in the global front. To prevent fabricated contributions, population states across generations are linked through hash chains. The correctness of the zk-NSGA-II protocol is first evaluated on twelve problems from the ZDT and DTLZ benchmarks. Second, its feasibility is shown in a real-world portfolio optimization case study with the Hang Seng dataset. Performance on these problems is measured with nine different metrics across three complementary perspectives for optimization, zero-knowledge proof and blockchain. The empirical findings indicate that trustless collaborative multi-objective optimization is feasible with the existing zero-knowledge and blockchain technologies.</p></details> |  |
| **[Topology as a Design Variable: Joint Architecture-Sizing Optimization for SRAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09248v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>SRAM's power-performance-area-stability trade-off depends on bitcell topology, a discrete architectural choice that interacts non-separably with continuous transistor sizing. However, no open-source SRAM tool treats topology as an optimizable variable. OpenRAM generates SRAM layouts but performs no optimization, while OpenYield supports high-fidelity evaluation but restricts optimization to scalar sizing of a fixed 6T cell. OpenYieldV2 closes this gap through three integrated contributions. First, a fully parameterized compiler generates complete 6T and 10T SRAM macros with peripheral circuits and replica-bitline self-timing, enabling topology comparison under identical simulation conditions. Second, a few-shot TabPFN surrogate learns topology-dependent PPA responses with limited full-macro SPICE samples. Third, a coarse-to-refined multi-objective optimizer combines global exploration of discrete architectural choices with local refinement of continuous transistor sizes. Experiments across varying design conditions reveal a topology decision boundary that shifts with operating constraints rather than following a fixed design rule. Jointly optimizing across this boundary improves hypervolume by up to 17.3\% over the strongest single-topology baseline. OpenYieldV2 is open-source to support reproducible SRAM design research.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ASPDAC2027</p></details> |

We argue that the harness, the Python code surrounding the model that constructs prompts, routes calls, and parses outputs, is a first-class design surface whose quality is inherently multi-objective: an accurate harness that refuses no unsafe request, or that consumes an order of magnitude more tokens, is not a good harness. While several methods to combine gradients already exist in the literature, they generally struggle when the objectives conflict. In contrast, we propose projecting gradients to fully resolve conflict. We prove convergence to the Pareto front in the smooth convex case with this approach, a stronger guarantee than the usual convergence to a weakly Pareto stationary point. Our method also enables instance-wise risk minimization (IWRM), a novel learning paradigm in which the loss of each training example is considered a separate objective. Early experimentation on image classification shows promising results for IWRM. Lastly, we provide an efficient implementation of JD using the Gramian of the Jacobian matrix to drastically reduce memory requirements.</p></details> | <details><summary>53 pa...</summary><p>53 pages, 14 figures, Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster)</p></details> |

The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. After normalization by the total length, it defines a cumulative distribution function (CDF) on weights whose inverse selects points at equal arc-length intervals. Building on this insight, we propose SURF (Sampling Uniformly along the Pareto Front). For structured problems, including bi-objective bandits, we derive analytical expressions for this CDF map and the resulting PF-aware weight sampling rule. Heuristic methods offer low computational overhead for relatively stable scheduling conditions, whereas metaheuristic methods are suited to complex and multi-objective optimization problems. Learning-based methods provide greater adaptability to dynamic and uncertain environments but introduce data, training, inference, and convergence requirements. Hybrid methods combine complementary capabilities but generally increase computational and implementation complexity. Based on these findings, a decision framework is developed to guide technique selection according to workload characteristics, environmental conditions, optimization objectives, resource constraints, and computational requirements.</p></details> |  |

| **[SIMS: Scale-Invariant Merit-Function-Based Scalarization for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12599v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-task learning (MTL) requires navigating unavoidable trade-offs among competing objectives. We empirically find that existing merit-function-based scalarization approaches are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives in practical MTL, where task losses commonly differ by orders of magnitude. The optimization process often favors objectives with larger scales even though the underlying Pareto optimal solutions remains invariant to rescaling (i.e., multiplying an objective by a positive constant). To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. Specifically, SIMS adopts a transformation-induced merit function to convert the MOO problem of MTL to a single objective that renders optimization invariant to the magnitudes of losses. Theoretically, we prove that the requirement for scale invariance uniquely determines this transformation to be logarithmic. We further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error. Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the 32nd ACM SIGKDD Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (KDD 2026)</p></details> |
| **[ParetoTransport: Generative Optimization by Mass Transport Toward The Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07706v1)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline multi-objective optimization requires not only moving the objective vectors of candidate designs toward the Pareto front, but also distributing them effectively along it. ParetoTransport guides a flow-matching sampler to iteratively transport the empirical offline distribution toward the Pareto front, with Wasserstein matching to intermediate proxy distributions. This directly controls distributional displacement and mass allocation along the front. We establish a convergence result and demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on standard offline MOO benchmarks, extending recent evaluations beyond hypervolume to generational distance, inverted generational distance, and Wasserstein distance.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Construct Practical Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00189v2)** | 2026-09-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated design and optimization of agentic LLM-based systems leads to sophisticated systems that substantially improve result quality over off-the-shelf agentic patterns. We then propose novel learning methods for the agentic components required by this framework, namely pseudo-tools and fixed workflows. These learning methods generally outperform hand-engineered agents. We also exploit the modularity of the framework to apply multi-objective optimization methods to jointly optimize cost and response quality and blend the results of multiple learning systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Interpretable and Fair Generalized Additive Neural Networks via Multi-objective Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05946v1)** | 2026-09-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interpretability and fairness are two of the most emphasized dimensions in trustworthy artificial intelligence (AI). To address this gap, this paper introduces explicit quantitative metrics for evaluating the interpretability of NN-based GAMs, empirically examines their effectiveness, and explores strategies for improving interpretability within these models. In addition, the simultaneous and explicit optimization of both interpretability and fairness, along with their trade-offs and the underlying reasons, remains underexplored. To address this, we propose a multi-objective neural basis model (MONBM) framework based on multi-objective evolutionary learning to consider accuracy, interpretability, and fairness simultaneously. A partial retraining strategy is further developed to facilitate the practical application of evolutionary multi-objective optimization to deep model architectures. Based on MONBM, this paper reveals the complex relationships between these dimensions and the reasons behind these intricate relationships. This analysis demonstrates how multi-objective optimization can be combined with self-interpretable models to reveal relationships among trustworthiness objectives. In addition, MONBM obtains a set of models with different trade-offs between dimensions, and the competitiveness of the approach is validated by comparing it with state-of-the-art methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in Neural Networks</p></details> |
| **[LLM4AIGQ: LLM-based AI Guidance Query Generation Framework for Multi Interest Mining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03674v1)** | 2026-09-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Guidance queries stimulate user consumption by extracting preferences to provide search queries with guidance value, playing a crucial role in the e-commerce field. Traditional AI-generated queries (AIGQ) generation primarily relies on a two-stage "Query-to-AI-Generated-Query" (Q2AIGQ) association paradigm, first recalling user primary search queries from user profiles, historical behavior sequences, item-side information, and the current query through multi-path retrieval, then generalizing AIGQ via rule-based methods. This approach suffers from semantic drift due to information cascade loss; additionally, primary search query derivation heavily depends on "user-item" co-occurrence relationships, lacking exploration of user multi-interests, resulting in guidance queries with low value and mismatched purchase intent. To address the expressive limitations of traditional co-occurrence-based retrieval, we propose LLM4AIGQ, an LLM-based solution for generating AI guidance queries tailored to users' multi-interests. This approach segments user interests by integrating user profiles and historical interaction sequences, infers specific consumption intents for each sub-interest, and subsequently generates corresponding AIGQ. In terms of model training, we employ a post-training pipeline comprising Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Reinforcement Learning (RL), and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to enhance the model's capability in generating AIGQ. We also introduce a multi-level reward design to satisfy the requirements of multi-objective optimization and long-chain reasoning in practical applications. Regarding deployment, we adopt a nearline-generation and online-read architecture to meet latency constraints. | **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
