| **[Trajectory Inference of Human Aging from Cross-Sectional DNA Methylation Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06583v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA methylation (DNAm) serves as one of the most robust molecular biomarkers of biological aging. While conventional epigenetic clocks accurately predict chronological age from high-dimensional CpG profiles, they treat aging as a static regression task, meaning they can only output a single score rather than simulating how an entire profile continuously changes over time. To reconstruct these continuous dynamics, we frame lifelong human epigenetic aging as a trajectory inference problem across discrete age snapshots derived from widely available cross-sectional data. We introduce a two-stage computational pipeline: first, an age-regularized Variational Autoencoder (VAE) maps high-dimensional CpG profiles onto a chronologically ordered latent manifold while preserving a generative decoder bridge back to the original methylation space. Second, we model the continuous movement across this latent space via Regularized Unbalanced Optimal Transport (RUOT) that unifies deterministic drift, random diffusion, and non-conservative mass changes. By resolving this RUOT formulation using the DeepRUOT framework, our model fluidly accommodates population-level density shifts like survivorship bias and cellular attrition without requiring rigid biological priors. Evaluated on a large-scale, 80-year pan-tissue dataset, our model demonstrates robust distribution interpolation and uncovers a prominent late-life surge in the learned growth field that mathematically captures the variance expansion driven by stochastic epigenetic drift. Finally, by decoding continuous latent paths back to individual CpG sites, we reconstruct and empirically verify distinct biological aging archetypes, offering a rigorous, generative paradigm for simulating human molecular aging.</p></details> | 16 pages, 7 figures |
| **[DNA-MGC+: A versatile codec for reliable and resource-efficient data storage on synthetic DNA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14527v3)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The biochemical processes underlying DNA data storage, including synthesis, amplification, and sequencing, are inherently noisy, leading to insertion, deletion, and substitution errors as well as sequence dropouts that challenge reliable data retrieval. Here we introduce DNA-MGC+, a DNA storage codec designed for reliable and resource-efficient retrieval under diverse operating conditions. We evaluate DNA-MGC+ across various in silico and in vitro settings. In comparison with selected representative codecs, the results show that DNA-MGC+ consistently improves key performance metrics under explicit reliability constraints, including required sequencing depth, read cost, decoding time, storage density, and error-correction capability. In silico experiments show that DNA-MGC+ supports reliable decoding at error rates up to 24% for a 15-KB file, while separate scalability experiments demonstrate reliable decoding of files up to 200 MB at a 5% error rate. In vitro experiments involving a 24-KB file and electrochemical synthesis show that different DNA-MGC+ configurations achieve minimum sequencing depths below 3x and read costs below 3.5 nts/bit under both Illumina and Nanopore sequencing.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted in Communications Engineering (Nature Portfolio)</p></details> |
| **[GeneZip: Region-Aware Compression for Long Context DNA Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17739v4)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-context DNA modeling is computationally expensive, motivating existing approaches to reduce sequence length through uniform downsampling. However, functional information and sequence redundancy are unevenly distributed across the genome, calling for finer resolution in functionally dense regions and stronger compression of redundant sequence. We introduce GeneZip, a region-aware DNA compression framework that learns where to allocate a limited token budget. Building on H-Net-style dynamic routing, GeneZip combines a Region-Aware Ratio objective, which uses regional labels derived from GENCODE gene annotations to specify compression targets during training, with bounded routing for stable token counts. At inference, it compresses raw DNA without annotations. GeneZip provides three main benefits. First, it preserves predictive utility under high compression: GeneZip-70M achieves 137.6-fold compression with a validation perplexity increase of 0.31 relative to uncompressed Mamba2-84M, and the best average rank across four DNALongBench tasks among compression models with leakage-controlled pretraining. Second, it learns region-aware allocation with emergent repeat-aware compression. Alternative regional priors yield task-dependent performance trade-offs. Without repeat-specific supervision, GeneZip compresses transposable-element-derived and tandem repeats more strongly than matched non-repeat sequence; its tandem-repeat compression preference transfers to a nine-species evaluation panel without further training. Third, it enables efficient long-context modeling: on 450-kb inputs, GeneZip-70M achieves 36.1-fold higher inference throughput than JanusDNA-7.7M on a single H100 GPU.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://andyjzhao.github.io/GeneZip/</p></details> |
| **[Pruning DNA Sequence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10386v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data pruning can substantially reduce training cost by removing examples that contribute little additional learning signal, but it has not been studied for DNA language-model pretraining. We introduce Sequence Pruning (SP), a dynamic pruning framework that removes genomic sequences as they become easy for the model to predict. SP uses per-sequence pretraining accuracy computed directly from the model's forward pass, requiring no separate scoring model or additional pass over the corpus. We evaluate SP across five pretraining settings spanning two corpora, three model families, and both MLM and CLM objectives, with downstream evaluation on five genomic benchmarks. SP reduces pretraining data processing by up to 96% while largely preserving downstream performance. At moderate savings, pruning improves performance by up to 1.2 MCC points over full-data pretraining; at the highest savings, pretraining uses only 4% of the baseline GPU hours with little performance loss. SP also outperforms the dynamic pruning methods InfoBatch and BPAS across most comparisons. Beyond accuracy, our framework supports alternative criteria such as perplexity and EL2N, although accuracy yields the strongest downstream performance at matched savings. These results show that genomic pretraining does not require repeatedly processing all available sequences and that dynamic sequence-level pruning can substantially improve its data and computational efficiency. Code is available at https://github.com/FaiasPromit/pruning-dna-sequence.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 7 figures, 18 tables</p></details> |
| **[Markov State-Space Modeling and Channel Characterization for DNA-Based Molecular Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23394v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA-based molecular communication with microarray-style reception under reversible hybridization is of interest for emerging DNA-enabled sensing and Internet of Bio-Nano Things (IoBNT) systems. To capture these effects in a communication-oriented form, we develop a Markov state-space framework based on a voxelized reaction-diffusion model, in which a block-structured transition matrix describes molecular transport and binding/unbinding dynamics. For the microarray specialization, this representation yields the channel impulse response (CIR), the equilibrium gain, and a settling-time-based characterization of the effective channel memory. Building on the resulting symbol-rate observation model for on-off keying, we derive a grouped-binomial counting model and obtain a closed-form expression for the covariance of the counting noise. Based on these statistics, we further develop a differential-threshold detector and a finite-memory decision-feedback equalizer and benchmark them against a symbol-by-symbol maximum a posteriori detector with decision feedback (MAP-DF). Numerical results validate the theoretical correlation behavior and show that the relative performance of the proposed receivers depends strongly on the channel-memory regime.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in IEEE Internet of Things Journal. 25 pages, includes supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[BEND 2.0: Benchmarking the Capabilities Needed to Design DNA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03806v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genomic foundation models are increasingly used to propose DNA sequences and edits. But successful design requires capabilities that current benchmarks do not measure. Across eight tasks built on five experimental datasets, we find that model choice and how representations are accessed both affect performance. Linear readouts fitted to frozen representations can match or exceed models' native outputs, and functional post-training substantially improves the models' capabilities. Linear readouts transfer well across experimental assays and genes, suggesting that models capture functional patterns beyond the data used to fit individual predictors. At the same time, strong prediction of average effects can conceal weaker prediction of context-specific effects, and accurate prediction of combined mutation effects does not imply accurate prediction of their interactions. In design tasks, model predictions can reduce the number of candidates needed to find a desired response, but still leave substantial room for better design choices, particularly for context-specific and multi-cell objectives. Our benchmark reveals that genomic models now display signs of general utility but have yet to saturate on performance in even simple tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Leade...</summary><p>Leaderboard on https://machinelearninglifescience.github.io/BEND2 and code on https://github.com/machinelearninglifescience/BEND2</p></details> |
| **[Searching for related contributors to a DNA mixture using single cell analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01782v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose a staged process for assessing the presence of related individuals in an autosomal STR DNA mixture sample, when it is analysed using a single cell pipeline analysis (as opposed to a bulk mixture analysis). | **[IsoAlign: Isoform-Aware DNA-Protein Alignment via Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00241v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genomic foundation models (GFMs) have emerged as a powerful paradigm for learning DNA sequence representations, yet existing self-supervised pretraining strategies overlook alternative splicing and underperform compared to specialized models. | **[Locality-Controlled Out-of-Distribution Guidance for Robust Regulatory DNA Sequence Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41736v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing regulatory DNA sequences for targeted gene-expression control is an important challenge. We postulate that this gap arises because prior methods treat guidance primarily as a matter of magnitude. In regulatory DNA, however, sequence grammar is spatially nonuniform: motif positions are sensitive to perturbation, while background regions allow more exploration. We therefore propose LOCOGEN by reformulating guidance as a problem of locality. LOCOGEN learns an invariant motif mask through sequence-level invariance learning to protect motif positions, while applying OOD damping only to background positions. It operates at sampling time on a frozen diffusion backbone, without retraining the backbone. Across HepG2, K562, and SK-N-SH, LOCOGEN reaches this previously underexplored region of the property-novelty-plausibility space. | **[MacKay-Neal Codes for DNA Channels under Block-MAP Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38440v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We prove achievability below the symmetric information rate (SIR) under block maximum a posteriori (MAP) decoding for a fixed-degree MacKay-Neal (MN) socket ensemble on the quaternary insertion-deletion-substitution channel used to model DNA reads. | **[Peak-DNA: Recurrent Morphology in Global Narrative Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Global narrative streams exhibit noisy, fragmented, and asynchronous amplification bursts, making direct waveform comparison unreliable for recurrence analysis. | **[CCP-DP: Cross-cluster context-guided phase dynamic programming for DNA storage sequence reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36559v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivation: DNA data storage offers high information density and long-term stability, but accurate sequence reconstruction remains challenging under limited sequencing coverage and strong insertion, deletion, and substitution (IDS) noise. | **[ACOR: incremental evidence accumulation towards real-time DNA storage reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36500v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA offers high-density, durable storage by encoding digital information into synthetic strands and retrieving it through sequencing. | **[The Narrative Gap: Can LLMs Help Us Navigate Diverse Narratives Across Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12750v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly mediate how people seek and consume information, including accounts that differ across languages and regions, especially on contested topics. Supporting diverse information seeking requires models to help users navigate competing narratives across languages and information streams. We define six capabilities necessary for diverse multilingual information seeking: three foundational capabilities for narrative inclusion, narrative exclusion, and missing narrative identification; and three higher-level capabilities for presenting diverse narratives, preserving narrative coverage under confirmatory queries, and maintaining information parity across languages. To guide the development of those information seeking capabilities, we introduce \textsc{NarrativeBench}, a benchmark comprising 1,333 news articles in 23 publication languages across 23 contested topics and 460 Reddit-grounded queries. Evaluating 14 LLMs across 24 query languages, we find that current models lack the capabilities needed to reliably navigate diverse narratives, limiting users' control over the perspectives they receive and leaving substantial gaps in exposure to competing accounts even when diversity is explicitly requested. Information access also differs across query framings and languages, risking echo chambers, linguistic filter bubbles, and information disparities that weaken the common ground needed for democratic discourse. \textsc{NarrativeBench} provides a foundation for developing information systems that give users across languages and regions agency to navigate diverse narratives.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Evaluations and Dataset Track</p></details> |

This version reports a preregistered live revalidation that replaces the static package lists of earlier versions with two independent checks against the live PyPI and npm registries. Hallucination rates are 3.6-5.0% of package mentions (2.2-3.4% of generations): the 2026 frontier sits at the level of 2024's commercial models, and the open-weight gap seen in 2024 has disappeared. Python exceeds JavaScript for every model. The set of names hallucinated by all five models shrinks from the 127 reported earlier to 104; 26 earlier entries were correct import statements whose module name differs from the package name. Using the accidental six-month public exposure of our own list as a natural experiment, we find no detectable effect of publication: about 1% of hallucinated names were registered within six months, at the same rate for published and unpublished names, and the 95% interval excludes increases above 2.5 percentage points. We apply it to adversely classified instances from four datasets in credit scoring, education and employment, generating narratives with five LLMs, and evaluate faithfulness, feature coverage and sentiment. We find significant disparities across all datasets, metric families and models, arising in two ways. Faithfulness disparities result from feature values: LLMs make plausible but false statements, triggered by values that are unequally distributed across social groups. Controlled simulations and MMLU-Pro tuning experiments show that winner-based reporting can be optimistic and can change deployment conclusions, while SIREN remains close to the finite-sample reporting target.</p></details> | <details><summary>The 4...</summary><p>The 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Understanding the Challenges in Iterative Generative Optimization with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23994v3)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative optimization uses large language models (LLMs) to iteratively improve artifacts (such as code, workflows or prompts) using execution feedback. It is a promising approach to building self-improving agents, yet in practice remains brittle: despite active research, only 9% of surveyed agents used any automated optimization. We argue that this brittleness arises because, to set up a learning loop, an engineer must make "hidden" design choices: What can the optimizer edit and what is the ``right'' learning evidence to provide at each update? We investigate three factors that affect most applications: the starting artifact, the credit horizon for execution traces, and batching trials and errors into learning evidence. Through case studies in MLAgentBench, Atari, and BigBench Extra Hard, we find that these design decisions can determine whether generative optimization succeeds, yet they are rarely made explicit in prior work. Different starting artifacts determine which solutions are reachable in MLAgentBench, truncated traces can still improve Atari agents, and larger minibatches do not monotonically improve generalization on BBEH. We then study these factors jointly in harness optimization on TBLite, evaluated on Terminal-Bench 2.0. We also propose a batch-size and credit-horizon rule that, calibrated on TBLite, transfers to DeepSWE after adjusting to its token costs and comes within 1% of the best configuration tested at each context budget.</p></details> | <details><summary>43 pa...</summary><p>43 pages. Accepted for NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Incorporating Machine-Others: An Enactive Exploration of Human-LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39869v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>New concepts based in interactional thinking are required to understand how humans are changed by relating to LLMs. Standard theories of technological impact upon users' autonomy are inadequate here, because this technology is capable of inducing linguistic interactions with humans in an unprecedented manner. Traditional theories of social cognition also fall short, because LLMs have neither autonomy nor minds nor sociality as such. And yet, humans interact, talk, and create with them. We base ourselves on enactive theory, particularly on the theory of Linguistic Bodies and recent developments of enactive accounts of AI, to explore how we make sense with machines. We argue that we incorporate machine-others in two ways: in the tool-like sense of incorporation as enabling particular skills, but also in the more linguistic and identity-tied sense of incorporating (and incarnating) LLMs' ways of languaging. However, LLMs are themselves excorporated and excarnated phantoms of our own languaging; we give a (machinic) body to our language. As such, we are not just incorporating machinic others; we are incorporating excorporated others. These conceptual advances draw a new map of our linguistic relation to LLMs, putting them in continuation with other forms of languaging involving other linguistic artifacts (e.g., religious books). We attend to some particular forms that this relation takes today, and how they may be impacting the autonomy and becoming of LLM-engaged humans. We introduce the idea of "susceptive sense-making" to characterize increased semiotic labour that is carried by the lack of friction that we sometimes encounter within these services.</p></details> |  |
| **[Semantic Synthesis over Brute-Force Search: LLM-Guided Transformation Design for Efficient Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10940v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data transformation pipelines shape the training distribution in computer vision, but designing them is a computationally expensive problem. We investigate whether large language models can enable efficient discovery by synthesizing executable transformation programs outside predefined augmentation spaces. We compare nine strategies under a unified training protocol, including standard augmentation policies, brute-force search, direct code generation with two LLMs, and performance-guided generation using LLMs fine-tuned on accuracy-ordered transformation pairs. When the best candidates are retrained for 20 epochs, LLM-generated transformation programs lead on every dataset, reaching 0.8877 accuracy on CIFAR-10, 0.5825 on CIFAR-100, and 0.9588 on SVHN, surpassing brute-force search over 6,000 candidates. The best transform on CIFAR-10 and SVHN comes from a fine-tuned generator using 270 candidates, while the best on CIFAR-100 comes from an off-the-shelf generator using only 10. Beyond transformation discovery, we analyze the reliability of the reduced-budget evaluation commonly used for augmentation search. Correlating one-epoch selection scores with 20-epoch outcomes reveals strong, weak, and absent predictive relationships depending on the dataset and architecture, with correlations ranging from 0.756 to near zero. A positive control indicates that range restriction alone is unlikely to explain the low correlations. Our results indicate that semantic synthesis with code LLMs provides an efficient alternative to search-based transformation discovery and highlight the importance of validating evaluation fidelity when optimizing data transformations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learn-To-Learn on Arbitrary Textual Conditioning: A Hypernetwork-Driven Meta-Gated LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01973v4)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conventional LLMs may suffer from corpus heterogeneity and subtle condition changes. In this paper, we activate the meta-signal of $β$ within the SwiGLU blocks, resulting in a meta-gating mechanism that adaptively adjusts the nonlinearity of FFN. A hypernetwork is employed which dynamically produces $β$ on textual conditions, providing meta-controllability on LLMs. By testing on different condition types such as task, domain, persona, and style, our method outperforms finetuning and meta-learning baselines, and can generalize reasonably on unseen tasks, condition types, or instructions. Our code can be found in https://github.com/AaronJi/MeGan.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 16 figures. ICML2026</p></details> |
| **[Pole or Vector? Encoders Capture Bangla Compound Verbs, but LLMs Read Them Literally](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10909v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In Bangla, a main verb is often followed by one of a small set of vector verbs. These verbs lose their own meaning and add a shade of meaning instead, such as completion. For example, likhe phello means that someone finished writing, not that they wrote and threw something. The same two verbs can also describe two separate events, so only the context shows which meaning is meant. This makes compound verbs a hard test of how language models handle multiword expressions. We present BanglaCV-Probe, a benchmark of 17 vector verbs with 1,044 minimal pairs, 204 entailment items and 197 pairs of correct and incorrect combinations, all checked by native speakers. We test 26 models (encoders, LLMs and translation systems) on four skills: telling the two meanings apart internally, understanding what a vector adds, preferring correct combinations and keeping the vector's meaning in translation. Probes on five encoders tell the two meanings apart and still reach up to 93% accuracy when trained on pairs without surface cues. Prompted models rarely use this knowledge. Entailment accuracy stays between 59% and 81%. Several LLMs answer Yes or No to almost every item, and nine of ten models miss that someone who ate up the rice left none. Most instruction-tuned LLMs translate vectors literally in 15-20% of vector sentences and in up to 55% for mora (die). On correct combinations, a small Bangla-only model (88%) matches BLOOM-7B1 (87%) and both beat Qwen2.5-7B (74%). Data and code: https://github.com/ashuvo25/Pole_OR_Vector</p></details> |  |
| **[ComplLLM: Fine-tuning LLMs to Discover Complementary Signals for Decision-making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.19458v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent decision pipelines can outperform single agent workflows when complementarity holds, i.e., different agents bring unique information to inform a final decision. We propose ComplLLM, a post-training framework based on decision theory that fine-tunes a decision-assistant LLM to output signals that complement existing agent decisions, using complementary information as reward. We validate ComplLLM on synthetic and real-world tasks involving domain experts, demonstrating how the approach recovers known complementary information, produces plausible explanations of complementary signals to support downstream decision-makers, and improves human and LLM agent decision performance in controlled studies.</p></details> |  |
| **[Approximate Recall, Approximate Forecasts: Recall as a Diagnostic for LLM Forecasting Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10891v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit approximate recall: they retain coarse associations about past events while losing fidelity on specific details. Past research on LLMs has quantified different models' forecasting ability in real-world events using prediction markets, but has not studied how losing said fidelity impacts their forecasting abilities. For example: while an LLM correctly recalls that the answer to: ''Did Anna Sawai win the Best Drama Actress in the 76th Emmy Awards?'' is ''Yes,'' it incorrectly states the event date as September 22 rather than September 15. In this paper, we introduce a novel metric called the Recall Fidelity Score to study how imprecision in internalized knowledge propagates to forecasting capability on future events. On resolved historical markets, we find that higher local recall fidelity around semantically similar past events predicts lower forecasting error on resolved markets, and the same signal predicts smaller distance from open markets prices.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 7 figures (including appendix). Accepted as a Spotlight at the ICML 2026 Workshop on Forecasting as a New Frontier of Intelligence</p></details> |
| **[Designing LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems for Software Engineering Tasks: Quality Attributes, Design Patterns and Rationale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08475v3)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As the complexity of Software Engineering (SE) tasks continues to escalate, Multi-Agent Systems (MASs) have emerged as a focal point of research and practice due to their autonomy and scalability. Furthermore, through leveraging the reasoning and planning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs), the application of LLM-based MASs in the field of SE is garnering increasing attention. However, there is no dedicated study that systematically explores the design of LLM-based MASs, including the Quality Attributes (QAs) on which designers mainly focus, the design patterns used by designers, and the rationale guiding the design of LLM-based MASs for SE tasks. To this end, we conducted a study to identify the QAs that LLM-based MASs for SE tasks focus on, the design patterns used in the MASs, and the design rationale for the MASs. We collected 106 papers on LLM-based MASs for SE tasks as the source. Our study shows that: (1) Code Generation is the most common SE task solved by LLM-based MASs among ten identified SE tasks, (2) Functional Suitability is the QA on which designers of LLM-based MASs pay the most attention, (3) Role-Based Cooperation is the design pattern most frequently employed among 16 patterns used to construct LLM-based MASs, and (4) Improving the Quality of Generated Code is the most common rationale behind the design of LLM-based MASs. Building on the study results, we presented the implications for the design of LLM-based MASs to support SE tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>42 pa...</summary><p>42 pages, 3 images, 12 tables, Manuscript submitted to a Journal (2026)</p></details> |
| **[Enhancing Assessment of Self-Consistency in LLM Explanations using Perturbation Strength](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30849v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prior work has examined the self-consistency of LLM-generated explanations using surface-level perturbation methods. However, the strength of these perturbations is not explicitly measured and controlled. In this work, we propose an LLM-as-a-judge approach to measure perturbation strength in a unified manner across input and CoT perturbations. We then evaluate the self-consistency in explanations generated from various LLMs under controlled strength conditions, ensuring a fair comparison across perturbation types. Experiments show that our proposed LLM-based perturbation strength measure outperforms other embedding- and probability-based approaches and that input perturbations generally affect LLMs more strongly than CoT perturbations. Our work suggests that judgments about a model's self-consistency is fair only within the same perturbation type.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 10 figures, corrected typos, Accepted for publication in ALTA 2026</p></details> |
| **[The Surprising Effectiveness of Deleting Weights in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10866v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How little of a pretrained large language model (LLM) has to change for it to acquire new capabilities? We find that zeroing fewer than 0.05% of its weights, with no other modification, matches full fine-tuning (FFT) at scale across three LLM post-training regimes: reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, supervised fine-tuning, and on-policy distillation. Our method, Deletion Tuning, learns a binary mask over the gate projection of transformer blocks. Two practical advantages follow: the trained mask is hundreds to thousands of times smaller than the corresponding FFT checkpoint, and Deletion Tuning forgets far less, which addresses a central challenge in continual learning. Under reinforcement learning, its prior-task accuracy stays near the base level while FFT, LoRA, and random sparse fine-tuning lose up to 5.4 points. To understand why masking alone suffices, we analyze the geometry of the learned weight changes under reinforcement learning using stable rank, spectrum drift, and principal-weight overlap. Compared with LoRA and random sparse fine-tuning, Deletion Tuning is closest to FFT across the three measures.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[MultiTurnRoute: A Platform for Building and Evaluating Multi-Turn LLM Routers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10848v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In a multi-turn conversation, the choice of language model at one turn can affect which model should answer the next. The selected model's reply shapes the context for the next query, and its cached prompt prefix can reduce the cost of using that model again. We introduce MultiTurnRoute, a platform for building and evaluating multi-turn LLM routers. A shared interface gives built-in and user-defined routers the current query, dialogue history, and an estimate of each model's reusable prefix length. Our routing algorithm, Chain-of-Route (CoR), uses a learned gate over recent and recurrent dialogue history to predict response quality and estimates cost from each model's reusable prefix. MultiTurnRoute Arena ranks routers on recorded conversations and compares session quality and cost in interactive runs where a simulated user responds to each reply. On a benchmark drawn from WildChat and LMSYS-Chat-1M with per-turn quality annotations for five LLMs, CoR reduces the normalized cost required to match the strongest model's quality by up to 20.3% relative to the strongest routing baseline, while matching or improving peak response quality.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
